[Esther] [3]:[5] So when Haman understood that Mordecai did not bow down to him, he was greatly inflamed with wrath,
[Esther] [3]:[6] and determined to destroy all the Jews throughout the whole kingdom of Artaxerxes.
[Esther] [3]:[7] And having drawn up a decree in the twelfth year of the reign of Artaxerxes, he cast lots for one day after another and for one month after another so as to destroy the whole race of Mordecai in one day. And the lot having fallen on the fourteenth day of the month Adar,
[Esther] [3]:[8] he spoke to king Artaxerxes, saying, There is a nation scattered among the nations throughout thy whole kingdom, whose laws differ from all the nations. And as they disobey the king's laws and it is not expedient for the king to let them alone;
[Esther] [3]:[9] if it seemeth good to the king, let him pass a decree to destroy them and I will subscribe ten thousand talents of silver to the king's treasury.
[Esther] [3]:[10] Thereupon the king taking off his ring gave it to Haman to seal the writings against the Jews.
[Esther] [3]:[11] And the king said to Haman, Keep the money, and do with the nation what thou pleasest.
[Esther] [3]:[12] So the king's secretaries were convened on the thirteenth day of the first month, and they wrote as Haman directed to the generals and the governors in every province, from India to Ethiopia a hundred and twenty seven provinces, and to the chiefs of the nations according to their dialect in the name of king Artaxerxes,
[Esther] [3]:[13] and the decree was despatched by posts throughout the whole kingdom of Artaxerxes, to destroy the race of the Jews on a certain day of the twelfth month which is Adar, and to make spoil of their goods.
[Esther] [3]:[14] And copies of the decree were to be sent through every province and all the nations were ordered to be ready against that day.
[Esther] [3]:[15] And when the business was despatched at Susoi, the king and Haman sat down to revel, but the city was struck with consternation.
[Esther] [4]:[1] Now when Mordecai knew what was done, he rent his clothes and put on sackcloth and strewed himself with ashes, and running through the street of the city, he cried with a loud voice, A nation is to be cut off, which hath committed no fault.
[Esther] [4]:[2] But when he came to the king's gate, he stopped; for it was not lawful for him to enter the court in sackcloth and ashes.
[Esther] [4]:[3] And in every country where the writings were published there was a cry with lamentation and great grief among the Jews, and they put on sackcloth and ashes.
[Esther] [4]:[4] When the queen's maids and chamberlains came in and told her, she was troubled at the news, and sent to clothe Mordecai and to take away his sackcloth from him; but he would not be persuaded.
[Esther] [4]:[5] Then Esther called Achrathaius her chamberlain, who attended her, and sent him to learn from Mordecai an exact state of the matter.
[Esther] [4]:[7] Whereupon Mordecai told him what was done and the promise which Haman had made to the king of ten thousand talents to the royal treasury, that he might destroy the Jews;
[Esther] [4]:[8] and he gave him a copy of the decree for their destruction, which was published among the Susians to shew it to Esther, and told him that he charged her to go in and supplicate the king and to entreat him for the people, calling to mind the days of thy humiliation when thou wast brought up by my bounty. Seeing Haman who is second to the king, hath spoken against us to destroy us, call thou upon the Lord and speak to the king for us, to deliver us from death.
[Esther] [4]:[9] Accordingly Achrathaius went in and told her all these words.
[Esther] [4]:[10] Thereupon Esther said to him, Go to Mordecai and tell him,
[Esther] [4]:[11] that all the nations of the kingdom know, that no man or woman who shall go to the king into the inner court without being sent for, hath any security for life: he only can be saved to whom the king shall stretch forth the golden sceptre. Now I have not been called to go to the king these thirty days.
[Esther] [4]:[12] When Achrathaius delivered this message of Esther to Mordecai,
[Esther] [4]:[13] Mordecai said to him, Go say to her, Esther, do not flatter thyself that thou alone of all the Jews in the kingdom shall escape.
[Esther] [4]:[14] Be assured, that if thou neglectest the present opportunity, help and protection will come to the Jews from some other quarter; but thou and thy father's house shall be destroyed. Who knoweth but that for this very occasion thou hast been made queen.
[Esther] [4]:[15] Then Esther sent back the messenger to Mordecai, saying,
[Esther] [4]:[16] Go and assemble the Jews who are at Susoi and fast for me. You must neither eat nor drink for three days; night nor day; and as for me, I and my maids will fast likewise, and then I will go to the king contrary to law, though perhaps I must die.
[Esther] [4]:[17] So Mordecai went and did as Esther commanded him.
[Esther] [5]:[1] And on the third day Esther put on her royal apparel and stood in the inner court of the king's palace over against the king's house, when the king was sitting on his royal throne, in the royal house, over against the gate.
[Esther] [5]:[2] And when the king saw Esther standing in the court, she obtained favour in his sight, and the king held out to Esther the golden sceptre which was in his hand, and Esther drew near and touched the top of the sceptre.
[Esther] [5]:[3] And the king said, What would queen Esther? And what is thy petition? To the half of my kingdom it shall be granted thee.
[Esther] [5]:[4] Thereupon Esther said, To-day is my set day, if therefore it seemeth good to the king, let him come with Haman to the banquet which I will this day prepare.
[Esther] [5]:[5] And the king said, Call Haman quickly that we may do as Esther hath said. So they both went to the banquet which Esther had ordered.
[Esther] [5]:[6] And at the banquet the king said to Esther, What would queen Esther? Whatever thou askest shall be granted.
[Esther] [5]:[7] To which she replied,
[Esther] [5]:[8] If I have found favour in the sight of the king, Let the king come again with Haman to-morrow to the banquet which I will prepare for them, and to-morrow I will make my request and my petition.
[Esther] [5]:[9] Then Haman went out from the king overjoyed and gladdened, but upon seeing Mordecai the Jew in the court, he was inflamed with great indignation.
[Esther] [5]:[10] So going home, he called his friends and his wife Zosara,
[Esther] [5]:[11] and having displayed to them his riches and the honour which the king had conferred on him; how he had promoted him to the highest rank and made him ruler of the kingdom,
[Esther] [5]:[12] the queen, said he, invited none to the banquet with the king, but only me: and to-morrow I am invited:
[Esther] [5]:[13] but these things give me no pleasure, when I see Mordecai the Jew in the court.
[Esther] [5]:[14] Thereupon Zosara his wife and his friends said to him, Let a gallows fifty cubits high be made for thee, and early in the morning speak to the king and let Mordecai be hanged thereon, then go to the banquet with the king and take thy fill of joy. And the thing pleased Haman. So the gallows was got ready.
[Esther] [6]:[1] Now on that night the Lord removed sleep from the king, therefore he ordered his attendant to bring the book of daily records, and read to him;
[Esther] [6]:[2] and finding what was written concerning Mordecai, how he had informed the king of the king's two chamberlains, when they commanded the guard, and sought to lay hands on Artaxerxes,
[Esther] [6]:[3] the king said, What honour or favour did we confer on Mordecai? To which the king's servants replied, Thou hast conferred none.
[Esther] [6]:[4] And while the king was making inquiry concerning Mordecai's fidelity behold Haman was in the court. And the king said, Who is in the court? Now Haman had come to speak to the king, that he might order Mordecai to be hanged on the gallows which he had prepared.
[Esther] [6]:[5] So the king's servants said, Behold Haman is standing in the court, and the king said, Call him in.
[Esther] [6]:[6] And the king said to Haman, What shall I do to the man whom I wish to honour? Upon this Haman said to himself, Whom doth the king wish to honour but me?
[Esther] [6]:[7] So he said to the king, With regard to the man whom the king wisheth to honour,
[Esther] [6]:[8] Let the king's servants bring out the royal robes with which the king is arrayed, and the horse on which the king rideth,
[Esther] [6]:[9] and give them to one of the most honourable of the king's friends, and let him array the man whom the king loveth, and mount him on the horse, and make proclamation through the street of the city saying, Thus shall it be done to every man whom the king honoureth.
[Esther] [6]:[10] Then the king said to Haman, Thou hast spoken well, Do thou so to Mordecai the Jew, who is an attendant in the court. Let nothing be omitted of what thou hast spoken.
[Esther] [6]:[11] So Haman took the robe and the horse, and having arrayed Mordecai he mounted him on the horse, and went through the street of the city and made proclamation saying, Thus shall it be done to every man whom the king wisheth to honour.
[Esther] [6]:[12] Then Mordecai returned to the court, and Haman went home in grief with his head covered.
[Esther] [6]:[13] And when Haman told his wife and his friends all that had happened to him; his friends and his wife said, If Mordecai be of the race of the Jews thy humiliation before him is begun. Thou shalt continue falling and shalt not be able to have thy revenge of him; for with him is the living God.
[Esther] [6]:[14] And while they were speaking the chamberlains came to hasten Haman to the banquet which Esther had prepared.
[Esther] [7]:[1] And when the king came with Haman to banquet with the queen,
[Esther] [7]:[2] the king said to Esther the second day at the banquet, What is it queen Esther? What is thy request, and what thy petition? to the half of my kingdom it shall be granted thee.
[Esther] [7]:[3] Thereupon she in reply said, If I have found favour in the sight of the king, let my life be granted at my request, and my people at my petition;
[Esther] [7]:[4] for I and my people are sold to be destroyed. Had it been to be plundered and reduced to slavery—that we and our children should be bondmen and bondwomen, I would have dissembled hearing it, for the accuser is not worthy of the king's court.
[Esther] [7]:[5] And the king said, Who is he who hath dared to do such a thing?
[Esther] [7]:[6] To which Esther replied, The adversary is Haman, this bad man. At this Haman was struck with consternation for fear of the king and the queen.
[Esther] [7]:[7] Then the king rising from the banquet went into the garden, and Haman supplicated the queen, for he saw himself in a desperate situation.
[Esther] [7]:[8] And on the king's returning out of the garden as Haman had prostrated himself on the sopha to supplicate the queen, the king said, What! would he offer violence to my wife in my house? Upon hearing this Haman was confounded.
[Esther] [7]:[9] Then Bugathan one of the chamberlains said to the king, Behold Haman hath prepared a gallows for Mordecai who spoke for the king. There is at Haman's house a gallows actually erected fifty cubits high.
[Esther] [7]:[10] Thereupon the king said, Let him be hanged thereon. So Haman was hanged on the gallows which he had prepared for Mordecai, and the king's anger was appeased.
[Esther] [8]:[1] And on that same day the king made a present to Esther of all that belonged to Haman the accuser. And the king sent for Mordecai; (for Esther told him that he was her kinsman;)
[Esther] [8]:[2] and the king took the ring which he had taken back from Haman and gave it to Mordecai, and Esther set him over all that belonged to Haman.
[Esther] [8]:[3] Then she spoke to the king again and fell at his feet and besought him to avert the mischief of Haman, and what he had devised against the Jews.
[Esther] [8]:[4] And when the king stretched forth the golden sceptre to Esther,
[Esther] [8]:[5] she arose and stood near the king and said, If it seemeth good to thee, and I have found favour, let despatches be sent to reverse the letters sent by Haman, which were written to destroy the Jews who are in thy kingdom.
[Esther] [8]:[6] For how can I see the calamity of my people? or how can I survive the destruction of my kindred?
[Esther] [8]:[7] Thereupon the king said to Esther, If I have given thee all the substance of Haman; and to gratify thee have caused him to be hanged on a gallows, because he laid his hands on the Jews, what more dost thou desire?
[Esther] [8]:[8] Write ye yourselves what you please in my name, and seal it with my ring. For what is written by the king's order, and sealed with my ring cannot be reversed.
[Esther] [8]:[9] So the secretaries were convened in the first month which is Nisan, on the three and twentieth day thereof in the same year, and a letter was written to the Jews reciting all that had been given in charge to the lieutenants and to the chief governors of the provinces from India to Ethiopia, a hundred and twenty seven provinces, to every province as they could read it,
[Esther] [8]:[10] then it was written by the king's command and sealed with his ring, (and these letters were sent by posts)
[Esther] [8]:[11] that he ordered them to use their own laws in every city, and to assist each other, and to use their adversaries, and them who assaulted them as they pleased,
[Esther] [8]:[12] on a certain day, the thirteenth of the twelfth month Adar, throughout the whole kingdom of Artaxerxes—that these subsequent writings were to counterbalance the former letter;
[Esther] [8]:[13] and that these counterbalancing writings should be exposed to publick view throughout the whole kingdom, that the Jews might be ready against that day to combat their adversaries.
[Esther] [8]:[14] So the horsemen set out in all haste to execute the king's orders. And when the decree was published at Susoi,
[Esther] [8]:[15] Mordecai went forth, arrayed in a royal robe, wearing a crown of gold and a turban of purple cotton. And upon seeing him the inhabitants of Susoi rejoiced.
[Esther] [8]:[16] And among the Jews, there was light and joy in every city and province where the decree was published.
[Esther] [8]:[17] Wherever proclamation was made, there was joy and gladness among the Jews, feasting and mirth; so that many of the nations were circumcised and became Jews, for fear of the Jews.
[Esther] [9]:[1] For in the twelfth month, on the thirteenth of the month Adar, the letters of the king having arrived, they who assaulted the Jews on that day were destroyed.
[Esther] [9]:[2] For none withstood them and the terror of them continued;
[Esther] [9]:[3] for the great lords and petty princes, and the king's secretaries, honoured the Jews; for the dread of Mordecai fell upon them;
[Esther] [9]:[4] for the decree of the king had caused his name to be known throughout the whole kingdom.
[Esther] [9]:[6] Now in the city Susoi, the Jews slew five hundred men,
[Esther] [9]:[7] including Pharsanes and Delphon, and Phasga
[Esther] [9]:[8] and Pharadatha, and Barea and Sarbaka,
[Esther] [9]:[9] and Marmasima and Kuphaias, and Arsaius and Zabuthaias,
[Esther] [9]:[10] the ten sons of Haman of Amadathu the Bugaian, the enemy of the Jews, and rifled them.
[Esther] [9]:[11] On that very day, when a return was made to the king of the number slain at Susoi,
[Esther] [9]:[12] the king said to Esther, The Jews have slain in the city Susoi five hundred men, how then, thinkest thou, have they behaved in the rest of the kingdom! What therefore dost thou request farther, and it shall be granted thee?
[Esther] [9]:[13] Thereupon Esther said to the king, Let the Jews be allowed to use to-morrow in like manner, that they may hang up the ten sons of Haman.
[Esther] [9]:[14] Accordingly he granted them leave to do so, and ordered the bodies of the ten sons of Haman, to be thrown out to the Jews to be hanged up.
[Esther] [9]:[15] So the Jews at Susoi assembled on the fourteenth day of the month Adar, and slew three hundred men, but took no spoil.
[Esther] [9]:[16] Now the rest of the Jews who were in the kingdom, assembled and assisted each other, and had rest from their enemies, for they slew fifteen thousand of them on the thirteenth of the month Adar, but took no spoil.
[Esther] [9]:[17] And having rested on the fourteenth of the month, they kept it as a day of rest with joy and gladness.
[Esther] [9]:[18] But the Jews of Susoi having assembled on the fourteenth and then rested, kept the fifteenth with joy and gladness.
[Esther] [9]:[19] Therefore because the Jews, who were scattered through all the distant provinces, keep the fourteenth of the month Adar, as a holy day, with joy, sending portions to one another,
[Esther] [9]:[20] Mordecai wrote an account of these matters in a book, and sent it to all the Jews who were in the kingdom of Artaxerxes far and near,
[Esther] [9]:[21] to set apart as holy days and to keep both the fourteenth and the fifteenth of the month Adar,
[Esther] [9]:[22] for in those days the Jews had rest from their enemies: And with regard to the month Adar, in which they had a change from grief to joy and from sorrow to gladness, to keep the whole month as good days of weddings and joy, sending portions to their friends and to the poor.
[Esther] [9]:[23] And the Jews took this upon them. As Mordecai wrote to them
[Esther] [9]:[24] how Haman of Amadathu, the Macedonian, warred against them—as he made calculations and cast lots to destroy them,
[Esther] [9]:[25] and as he went to the king with an intention to hang Mordecai, but all the evils he endeavoured to bring on the Jews, fell upon himself, and he and his sons were hanged;
[Esther] [9]:[26] therefore these days were called Phrouri, because of the lots, which in their language are called Phrouri. On the account of the things contained in that letter and of all that they suffered in consequence thereof, and all that happened to them, as he instituted,
[Esther] [9]:[27] so the Jews took upon themselves and their posterity, and upon all that joined them, never to use them in any other manner.
[Esther] [9]:[28] Therefore let these days be a lasting memorial from generation to generation, in every city, country and province; and let these days of Phrouri be kept forever; and let the memorial of them never perish from among their generations.
[Esther] [9]:[29] Then Esther the queen, the daughter of Aminadab, and Mordecai the Jew, wrote all that they did, and a confirmation of the letter respecting the Phrouri,
[Esther] [9]:[31] which Mordecai and Esther the queen enjoined on themselves, to their own prejudice, having at that time established their counsel against their health.
[Esther] [9]:[32] Thus did Esther actually establish it, and it was written to be kept in remembrance.
[Esther] [10]:[1] Then the king laid a tribute upon the dominion both of the land and the sea.
[Esther] [10]:[2] But with regard to his power and his valour and the riches and the glory of his kingdom, behold they are written in the book of the kings of the Persians and the Medes to be kept in remembrance.
[Esther] [10]:[3] Now Mordecai was viceroy of king Artaxerxes, and was great in the kingdom, and being honoured by the Jews and beloved, he enforced the observance of their religion on all his nation.
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[1] The words of the preacher the son of David, king of Israel in Jerusalem.
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[2] Vanity of vanities, said the preacher. Vanity of vanities. All is vanity.
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[3] What lasting advantage hath a man by all the labour with which he toileth under the sun?
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[4] One generation passeth away and another cometh. But the earth hath continually kept its station.
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[5] The sun riseth and the sun setteth and cometh round to his place.
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[6] Rising there he marcheth southward, then wheeleth about to the north. The wind wheeleth in circuits, and in its circuits it returneth.
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[7] All the rivers run into the sea, yet the sea is not filled. To the place whence the rivers come, thither they return to continue their course.
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[8] All these considerations are wearisome. Man cannot recount them: nor can the eye be satisfied with seeing; nor the ear filled with hearing.
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[9] What is that which hath been? The very same which shall be. And what is that which hath been done? The very same which shall be done: for there is nothing entirely new under the sun.
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[10] Who can speak and say, Behold this is entirely new. It hath been already in the ages past which were before us.
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[11] There is no remembrance of former things: and of those things which shall happen hereafter, there will be no remembrance among them who shall be born at a remote period.
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[12] I the preacher was king over Israel in Jerusalem;
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[13] and I applied my heart to seek and make diligent search by wisdom concerning all the things which are done on the earth. Because God hath assigned to the sons of men painful employment, that they may be kept employed,
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[14] I took a view of all the different sorts of work which are done under the sun. And behold they are all vanity and vexation of spirit.
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[15] That which is crooked cannot be made straight, nor can wants be numbered.
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[16] I communed with my heart, saying, Behold I am become great and have made advances in wisdom above all who were before me in Jerusalem. And I have applied my mind to know wisdom and knowledge.
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[17] And my mind hath taken a view of many things—of wisdom and knowledge—of parables and science. I have found by experience that this also is indeed vexation of spirit.
[Ecclesiastes] [1]:[18] Because by much wisdom there is much knowledge; and he who addeth to his knowledge will add to his sorrow;
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[1] I said in my heart, Come now let me try thee with pleasure. Take a view of that considered as a good. But behold this also is vanity.
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[2] To laughter I said, It is madness; and to mirth, What is this thou art doing?
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[3] Now I had examined whether my mind could lead my flesh to wine; and my mind had led it with wisdom, retaining a command over pleasure, until I should see of what sort that good is, which the sons of men are to pursue under the sun, all the days of their life.
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[4] I enlarged my plan of operations. I built me houses. I planted me vineyards.
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[5] I made me gardens and orchards and planted therein all sorts of fruit trees.
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[6] I made me pools of water, to water from them my blooming nurseries.
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[7] I purchased men servants and maid servants; and had servants born in my family. And my stock of herds and flocks was great above all who were before me in Jerusalem.
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[8] I collected also for myself silver and gold and the choicest treasures of kings and countries. I got me men singers and women singers, and the delights of the sons of men—a butler and the requisites to entertainments.
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[9] And I was great and surpassed all who had been before me in Jerusalem; and still my wisdom continued with me.
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[10] And whatever mine eyes desired I withheld not from them, nor did I restrain my heart from all my pleasure. Because my heart had been gladdened with all my labour, (now this was my portion from all my labour)
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[11] therefore I looked back on all the works which my hands had done, and on the labour with which I had toiled to execute them; and behold all was vanity and vexation of spirit and there is no lasting advantage under the sun.
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[12] Then I looked back to take a view of wisdom and madness and folly. In all the particulars which formed this deliberation (for who is the man equal to a compleat investigation)
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[13] I saw indeed that there is an advantage in wisdom above folly, like the advantage of light above darkness.
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[14] The wise man's eyes are in his head; but the fool walketh in darkness. Nevertheless I knew that one event will happen to them all.
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[15] Therefore I said in my heart, As the same event will happen to me as happeneth to the fool, why have I studied wisdom? Why communed abundantly with my heart? Because even this is vanity; (since out of an abundance the fool speaketh)—
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[16] because there is no remembrance forever of the wise man more than of the fool (seeing the days are now coming when all shall be for gotten and the wise must die as well as the fool)
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[17] therefore I hated the whole of this life; because the work which was done under the sun was toilsome to me—because all are vanity and vexation of spirit:
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[18] and I hated all this labour of mine for which I am toiling under the sun; because I must leave it to a man who is coming after me;
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[19] and who knoweth whether he shall be a wise man or a fool? Now if he hath power over all my labour for which I have toiled and in which I have displayed wisdom under the sun; surely this is vanity.
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[20] Then I turned about that my heart might state distinctly in every labour of mine in which I had toiled under the sun;
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[21] that here is a man whose labour hath been with wisdom and with knowledge, and with ability; and there is a man to whom, though he did not labour fur him, he shall give his portion. Surely this is vanity and a great vexation—
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[22] That such is the case with another man by all his labours and the vexation of his heart with which he toileth under the sun,
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[23] that all his days are days of sorrow and his employment grief, and even at night his heart hath no rest. Surely this is vanity.
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[24] Hath not a man something good which he can eat and drink and which he can point out to his soul as a good by his labour?
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[25] With respect to that I saw indeed that even this is from the hand of God; for who can eat or who can drink without him?
[Ecclesiastes] [2]:[26] For to the man who is good in his sight he hath given wisdom and knowledge and joy; and to the sinner he hath given the trouble of gathering and heaping up to give to him who is good in the sight of God. So that this also is vanity and vexation of spirit.
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[1] Time is for all things: but there is a particular portion of time for every particular affair under heaven—
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[2] a time to be born and a time to die—a time to plant and a time to root up what was planted—
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[3] a time to kill and a time to heal—a time to pull down and a time to build up—
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[4] a time to weep and a time to laugh—a time to mourn and a time to dance—
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[5] a time to cast away stones and a time to gather stones together—a time to embrace and a time to refrain from embracing—
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[6] a time to seek and a time to lose—a time to keep and a time to throw away—
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[7] a time to rend and a time to sew—a time to be silent and a time to speak—
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[8] a time to love and a time to hate—a time of war and a time of peace.
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[9] What is the lasting advantage of the agent from the things in which he laboureth?
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[10] I have taken a collective view of all the business which God hath assigned to the sons of men to be exercised therewith.
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[11] All the things which he made, considered as a whole, are good in his time. But with regard to all things considered as one whole, to them he hath assigned the whole age; so that no man can find out the work which God hath done from beginning to end.
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[12] I have found that there is no good in them
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[13] (I mean in regard to every man who eateth and drinketh, and can see good in all his labour) if there is not a gift of God that he may be made glad and do good in his life.
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[14] I have found that all the things which God hath made shall continue their age; there is no adding to it nor taking from it; and that God hath acted that they may be awed at his presence.
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[15] What hath been is now; and what are to be have already been; and God will find out him who is persecuted:
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[16] and yet I saw under the sun a place of judgment; was the wicked there? and a place for the just; was the pious there?
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[17] Then I said in my heart God will judge the whole class of the righteous, and the whole class of the wicked. For there is a time for every thing; and he is there over all the work.
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[18] I communed with my heart concerning a saying of the sons of men, "That God distinguisheth them." Now in order to shew that they themselves are beasts,
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[19] that at least which befalleth man, befalleth them; and that which befalleth the beast befalleth man. The same event happeneth to both. As the one dieth, so doth the other. And they have all one breath. What advantage then hath man over the beast? None: for all are vanity.
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[20] They are all for one place. They were all from dust: and to dust they shall all return.
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[21] And who hath seen the breath of the sons of men whether it ascendeth upwards; and the breath of the beast, whether it descendeth downwards into the earth?
[Ecclesiastes] [3]:[22] So I saw that there is nothing good in the works of man, but that by which he can be made glad; for that is his portion, for who will bring him to see what will be after him?
[Ecclesiastes] [4]:[1] Then I turned and took a view of all the different sorts of oppression under the sun; and behold the tear of the oppressed and they have no comforter. Though from the hand of oppressors some have relief; yet others have no comforter.
[Ecclesiastes] [4]:[2] Therefore I praised the whole body of the dead—those who were already dead, above the living—those who are still alive:
[Ecclesiastes] [4]:[3] but better than both is he who hath never been—who hath not seen all the evil work which is done under the sun.
[Ecclesiastes] [4]:[4] In the review of labour in general, I took a view in particular of manly exertion in executing. Because this causeth a man to be envied by his neighbour, this therefore is vanity and vexation of spirit.
[Ecclesiastes] [4]:[5] The fool folded his hands and devouring his flesh said,
[Ecclesiastes] [4]:[6] "Better is one handful of rest than two handfuls of labour and vexation of spirit."
[Ecclesiastes] [4]:[7] Then I turned and took a view of a vanity under the sun;
[Ecclesiastes] [4]:[8] Here is a single individual who hath no second; at least he hath neither son nor brother, yet there is no end to all his labour. His eye is not satisfied with riches, yet doth he say, For whom do I labour and deprive my soul of good? Surely this is vanity and a sore vexation.
[Ecclesiastes] [4]:[9] two are better than one. They have a better reward for their labour.
[Ecclesiastes] [4]:[10] For if they chance to fall the one can raise up his fellow. But alas for him who is alone, when he falleth and hath not another to raise him up.
[Ecclesiastes] [4]:[11] Again if two lie together they have warmth: but how can one be warm who is alone?
[Ecclesiastes] [4]:[12] Again though one should be strong the two may withstand him, and a three fold cord cannot easily be broken.
[Ecclesiastes] [4]:[13] Better is a poor and wise subject than an old and foolish king who hath not yet learned how to conduct himself;
[Ecclesiastes] [4]:[14] for out of prison he may come to reign. Because even in his own kingdom he was needy,
[Ecclesiastes] [4]:[15] I took a view of the whole body of the living who were walking under the sun with the youth second in rank who is to succeed him.
[Ecclesiastes] [4]:[16] There is no end of all the people—of all who resorted to them. Yet they who come last will have no joy in him. Surely this also is vanity and vexation of spirit.
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[1] Keep thy foot when thou goest to the house of God, and let thy sacrifice be a readiness to hear rather than the oblation of fools. Because they are not sensible that they do amiss,
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[2] be thou not rash with thy mouth; nor let thy heart be hasty to utter a word in the presence of God. Because God is in the heaven above, and thou art on the earth, let thy words there fore be few.
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[3] Because a dream cometh by much temptation, and a fool's voice with many words;
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[4] when thou vowest a vow to God delay not the performance of it. For in fools there is no fixed will. Pay thou therefore what thou vowest.
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[5] It is better not to vow, than to vow and not perform.
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[6] Suffer not thy mouth to cause thy flesh to sin, nor say in the presence of God, It was an inadvertence, that God may not be provoked to wrath by thy voice, and destroy the works of thy hands,
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[7] because it was with a multitude of dreams and vanities, and many words that thou fearest God.
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[8] If thou seest a frivolous accusation of a poor man, and a rigorous exaction of judgment and justice in a country, marvel not at the matter. Because there is a high one over a high one to watch, and high ones are for these things,
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[9] and the profit of land is for every one, a king is appendant to the cultivated field.
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[10] A lover of silver cannot be satisfied with silver; but did any one ever love what the abundance of this produceth? Surely this is vanity.
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[11] By an abundance of wealth they who consume it are multiplied. And what is the mighty advantage of the owner from it? That he hath the first sight of it with his eyes?
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[12] The sleep of the slave is sweet whether he eateth little or much. But the fulness of the rich suffereth him not to sleep.
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[13] There is a weakness which I have seen under the sun: riches kept by one for his descendant to his own hurt.
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[14] Yet these riches acquired with grievous vexation shall perish, and he hath begotten a son, and there is nothing in his hand.
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[15] As he came naked out of his mother's womb, he shall turn about and go as he came. He shall take nothing by his labour to go with him in his hand.
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[16] Surely this a grievous weakness; for as he came, so he must depart. What then is his lasting advantage, for which he laboureth in vain;
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[17] and for which all his days are in darkness and mourning, and much grief and weakness and repining?
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[18] Behold I have seen a good which is comely, namely to eat and drink, and to have a good enjoyment in all the labour with which one toileth under the sun, the number of the days of his life which God hath given him; for this is his portion.
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[19] And every man indeed to whom God hath given riches and wealth, and granted him power to eat thereof, and to take his portion, hath by means of his being made glad in his labour, this further gift of God,
[Ecclesiastes] [5]:[20] that he will not much remember the days of his life, because God occupieth him with the joy of his heart.
[Ecclesiastes] [6]:[1] There is an evil which I have seen under the sun, and it is common among men—
[Ecclesiastes] [6]:[2] a man to whom God giveth riches and wealth and glory, so that he wanteth nothing for his soul of all that he can desire; but God granteth him not a power to eat thereof. In as much as a stranger is to devour it, this is vanity and grievous infirmity.
[Ecclesiastes] [6]:[3] Though a man should beget a hundred children and live many years, if during that multitude of years which his days shall continue, his soul shall not be satisfied with an enjoyment of good, and he hath got no burying place, I conclude that an Abortive is better than he.
[Ecclesiastes] [6]:[4] Grant that it came in vanity and goeth away in darkness; with darkness also his name shall be covered!
[Ecclesiastes] [6]:[5] It indeed never saw the light; and he never knew rest: it hath an advantage over him
[Ecclesiastes] [6]:[6] though he had lived the revolutions of a thousand years, if he never enjoyed good. Do not all go to one place?
[Ecclesiastes] [6]:[7] All the labour of a man is for the mouth; yet the appetite will not be satisfied:
[Ecclesiastes] [6]:[8] here then a wise man hath the advantage over a fool; since the poor man knoweth that to pass through life,
[Ecclesiastes] [6]:[9] what he sees with the eyes is better for him than to be wandering after appetite, which is indeed vanity and vexation of spirit.
[Ecclesiastes] [6]:[10] Whatever hath been is now called by its name, and it is known what man is who cannot contend with one who is stronger than he.
[Ecclesiastes] [6]:[11] Since there are many arguments to prove the abundance of vanity; is there any one thing better than another for man?
[Ecclesiastes] [6]:[12] Doth any one know what is good for man in this life? All the days of his life of vanity he hath indeed done these things under a shade; is there any who can tell him what will be after him under the sun?
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[1] A good name is better than precious oil; and the day of death, than a birth day.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[2] It is better to go to a house of mourning than to a house of feasting. As that is the end of every man therefore the living will improve his understanding.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[3] Grief is better than laughter; for by a sadness of countenance a heart may be made better.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[4] The heart of the wise is in a house of mourning; but the heart of fools, in a house of mirth.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[5] It is better for a man to hear the rebuke of the wise, than to listen to the song of fools.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[6] Like the crackling of thorns under a pot, so is the laughter of fools. Surely this also is vanity.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[7] Because oppression distracteth a wise man and destroyeth his good disposition, the final sentence is better than such a government.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[8] Better is a patient man than one of a haughty spirit.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[9] Be not apt to be grieved in spirit; for grief will rest in the bosom of fools.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[10] Say not, How happened it that the former days were better than these? For thou hast not inquired wisely concerning this.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[11] Wisdom is good with an inheritance; and it is an advantage to them who see the sun, that they are in the shade of it.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[12] Wisdom is like the shade of money; but the advantage of the knowledge of wisdom is—it will give life to him who is a follower of it.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[13] Contemplate the works of God; for who can make him straight whom God hath made crooked.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[14] In the days of prosperity enjoy good; but consider in the day of adversity—consider that God hath made the one in due proportion to the other, forming a whole, as hath been said, that man cannot find out any of his ways.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[15] In the days of my vanity I took a view of all classes. Here is a righteous man perishing for his righteous act: there a wicked man continuing in his wickedness.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[16] Be not over righteous; nor dive too far into wisdom, lest peradventure thou be struck with astonishment.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[17] Run not into an excess of wickedness nor become hardened, that thou mayst not die untimely.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[18] It is good to restrain thyself in the one case; and surely for the other consideration thou shouldst not pollute thy hand. Because to them who fear God all things may happen,
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[19] wisdom will help the wise man more than ten mighty men who are in the city.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[20] Because there is not a righteous man on earth who will do good and not commit sin,
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[21] therefore thou shouldst not pay too close attention to all the words which the wicked shall utter, that thou mayst not hear thy servant cursing thee.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[22] For many a time he may revile thee and in many ways vex thy heart, because thou perhaps in like manner didst curse others.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[23] Having proved all these things by wisdom, I said, Let me investigate the cause. But that was far removed from me; farther than it was.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[24] It is indeed a deep depth, who can find it out?
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[25] I took a circuit with my heart to know and examine carefully and to seek wisdom and a mode of calculating, and to gain a knowledge of a particular folly and disquietude and madness of one destitute of the fear of God; and I find and an declare,
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[26] That bitterer than death is that whole class of women who are prostitutes. Though their heart is nets and their hands chains, he who is good in the sight of God shall be delivered from them; but a sinner shall be taken by them.
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[27] Behold this I found (said the preacher)
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[28] examining them one by one to find a class which my soul earnestly sought, but I did not find: though I found one man of a thousand, yet a woman among all these I did not find: but behold this I found, that
[Ecclesiastes] [7]:[29] God made mankind upright; but they have found out many classes.
[Ecclesiastes] [8]:[1] Doth any one know wise men? Doth any one know the solution of a matter? Wisdom will make his countenance shine; but one of an unblushing countenance will be hated.
[Ecclesiastes] [8]:[2] Watch the mouth of a king, and in regard to the oath of God be not impatient.
[Ecclesiastes] [8]:[3] From his presence thou mayst withdraw. Stay not when he giveth wicked orders; for he will do what he pleaseth,
[Ecclesiastes] [8]:[4] as a king is absolute; and who can say to him, What dost thou?
[Ecclesiastes] [8]:[5] He who keepeth a command will not acknowledge it to be—a wicked order; but a wise man's heart knoweth the time of judgment, that—there is a time and a judgment for everything.
[Ecclesiastes] [8]:[6] Because the man's determination may be much against him—
[Ecclesiastes] [8]:[7] because none knoweth what may happen, who can tell him what may come to pass?
[Ecclesiastes] [8]:[8] No man is absolute commander over wind to stop the current of it, and over the day of death he hath no authority; nor is there a discharge in the day of battle; nor can wickedness save him who is under its sway.
[Ecclesiastes] [8]:[9] Having taken a view of this as a whole, I applied my heart to every work which is done under the sun—to all those things in which man exerciseth authority over man to his hurt.
[Ecclesiastes] [8]:[10] And I then saw wicked men carried to their graves, even out of this holy place; and when they were gone, they were praised in the city, because they had done as they did. Surely this is vanity.
[Ecclesiastes] [8]:[11] Because those who do evil are not speedily called to an account, therefore the heart of the children of men is fully set in them to do evil.
[Ecclesiastes] [8]:[12] He who sinned continued thenceforward to do evil, even because of the forbearance shewn to them. Now as I know that it is good for them who fear God, that they should be awed by his presence;
[Ecclesiastes] [8]:[13] and that it cannot be well with the wicked; and that he who is not awed by the presence of God cannot prolong his days at ease; this which is practised on earth is vanity.
[Ecclesiastes] [8]:[14] Because there are righteous men on whom cometh as it were the work of the wicked; and because there are wicked men on whom cometh, as it were, the work of the righteous, I said that this is also surely vanity;
[Ecclesiastes] [8]:[15] and I praised pleasurable enjoyment, because there is nothing good for men under the sun—nothing save eating and drinking and being made glad; and this one may have conjoined with his labour all the days of his life which God giveth him under the sun.
[Ecclesiastes] [8]:[16] In all those things to which I applied my heart to know wisdom and to take a view of the business which is done on the earth; because day and night this is never at rest,
[Ecclesiastes] [8]:[17] therefore took a view of all the works of God in classes; for man is incapable of finding out the whole system of things under the sun. Let a man labour ever so much in the search he cannot find it out; and let a wise man say ever so much of knowing he cannot find it; for this cause I laid this whole system before my heart, and my heart took a view of it.
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[1] As the righteous and the wise and their works are in the hand of God, man indeed knoweth not what to love or what to hate: with regard to all things before him there is vanity in them all.
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[2] The same event happeneth to the righteous and to the wicked; to the good, and to the bad; to the clean and to the unclean; to him who sacrificeth and to him who sacrificeth not: as is the good, so is the sinner; as is he who sweareth, so is he who feareth the oath.
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[3] There is this evil in every thing done under the sun, that one event happeneth to them all, so that the heart of the children of men being filled with evil there is an instability in their heart during their life, and it followeth them to the dead.
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[4] Because whoever is in society with all the living hath hope; because the dog which is alive is better than the lion which is dead—
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[5] because they who are alive must know that they shall die; but the dead are not sensible of any thing and there is no more a portion for them—because the remembrance of these is forgotten,
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[6] and their love and their hatred and their envy are already perished and they have no more forever a portion in any thing done under the sun;
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[7] come eat thy bread with cheerfulness, and drink thy wine with a good heart: because God hath approved of thy works,
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[8] let thy garments be always white; and let not oil be wanting on thy head:
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[9] enjoy life also with the wife whom thou lovest all the days of thy life of vanity which are given thee under the sun: for this is thy portion in this life of thine, and this thy labour with which thou toilest under the sun.
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[10] Whatever thy hand findeth to do, do it with all thy might; since there is no work, nor device, nor knowledge nor wisdom in the mansion of the dead to which thou must go.
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[11] Having turned about, I saw under the sun that the race is not to the swift, nor the battle to the mighty; nay not even bread to the wise, nor riches to men of understanding; nor favour to men of knowledge—that time and chance will happen to them all as forming one whole.
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[12] That man indeed no more knoweth his time than the fishes which are enclosed in an evil dredge, or than birds which are caught in a net. Like them the sons of men are drawn into an evil time when it cometh upon them suddenly.
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[13] This also I saw under the sun in regard to wisdom which with me is in high estimation.
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[14] There was a small city and few men in it. And there came against it a mighty king who besieged it and built great ramparts against it;
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[15] but he happened to find in it a poor wise beggar, and this man by his wisdom saved the city; yet no man remembered the class of this poor beggar.
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[16] Now I have said, Wisdom is better than power, yet the wisdom of this poor man is despised and his words are not regarded.
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[17] The words of wise men in a time of rest are more minded than the shout of men exercising authority in the bustles of folly.
[Ecclesiastes] [9]:[18] Wisdom is better than implements of war. One sinner indeed will destroy much good.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[1] Dead flies will spoil the best prepared perfumes: but the smallest portion of wisdom is of more value than the greatest glory of folly.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[2] The heart of a wise man is on his right: but the heart of a fool is at his left.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[3] In every way at least when a fool is on his march his heart will fail him so that all which he shall devise is folly.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[4] If the spirit of a ruler rise up against thee, resign not thy place: for a reconciliation will mollify great offences.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[5] There is an evil which I have seen under the sun as if something involuntary came forth from the presence of a person in authority.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[6] The fool is set in high stations while the rich shall sit in a low place.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[7] I have seen slaves on horseback and princes walking like slaves on the ground.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[8] He who diggeth a pit may fall into it; and him, who is clearing away a hedge, a serpent may bite.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[9] He who raiseth stones high may be overpowered by them; and he who cleareth wood will thereby incur danger.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[10] If the axe shall fall, or one blunteth its edge and redoubled force is to be applied; the advantage will be in favour of the man who hath skill.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[11] If a serpent biteth at an interval of charming, there is then no advantage in him who charmeth.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[12] The words of a wise man's mouth are a charm; but the lips of a fool will swallow up himself.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[13] The beginning of the words of his mouth is folly and the end of his talk is malicious madness.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[14] Though the fool multiplieth words, a man doth not know what the matter is; and what that shall be which will follow who can tell him.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[15] The labour of the foolish will tire them like that of one who knoweth not the way to a city.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[16] Alas for thee, city, whose king is a novice and whose chiefs eat in the morning.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[17] Happy thou, land, whose king is a son of freemen and whose chiefs eat in due season for strength: and that they may not be put to shame.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[18] By slothfulness the roof will fall, and by idleness of hands a house will become leaky,
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[19] so as to excite a derision of them who are providing bread and wine and oil that they may be regaled while living. But to the subjection of money all things will be obedient.
[Ecclesiastes] [10]:[20] Therefore curse not a king in thy mind, and in the inmost recesses of thy bed chamber curse not a rich man: for a bird of the air will carry thy voice, and that which hath wings will tell thy saying.
[Ecclesiastes] [11]:[1] Send away thy bread on the face of the water; for after many days thou shalt find it.
[Ecclesiastes] [11]:[2] Give a portion to seven and even to eight; for thou knowest not what evil may be on the earth.
[Ecclesiastes] [11]:[3] When the clouds are filled with rain they empty themselves on the earth: and if a tree falleth, whether it be to the south or north, in the place where it shall fall there it shall be.
[Ecclesiastes] [11]:[4] He who observeth the wind will not sow; and he who looketh at the clouds will not reap.
[Ecclesiastes] [11]:[5] In these cases there is no knowing which way the wind will blow. As thou knowest not how bones are formed in the womb of her who is with child; so thou canst not know the works of God—all that he shall do forming one system.
[Ecclesiastes] [11]:[6] In the morning sow thy seed; and in the evening let not thy hand forbear; for thou knowest not which will succeed, whether this or that; and should both prosper alike, they are good.
[Ecclesiastes] [11]:[7] Light is indeed sweet, and it is grateful to the eyes to view the solar system;
[Ecclesiastes] [11]:[8] yet because, though a man may live many years and be delighted in them all, he will still remember that the days of darkness are many; that all that is coming is vanity;
[Ecclesiastes] [11]:[9] rejoice then, young man, in thy youth, and let thy heart cheer thee in the days of thy youth, and walk in the ways of thy heart spotless and not by the sight of thine eyes, and know that for all these things God will bring thee to judgment.
[Ecclesiastes] [11]:[10] Therefore remove wrath from thy heart and put away evil from thy flesh.
[Ecclesiastes] [12]:[1] Because youth and thoughtlessness is vanity, therefore remember thy Creator in the days of thy youth; before the evil days come and the years draw nigh in which thou shalt say, I have no pleasure in them.
[Ecclesiastes] [12]:[2] So long as the sun and the light and the moon and the stars are not involved in darkness, the clouds will return after rain.
[Ecclesiastes] [12]:[3] When once the guards of the house are shaken, and the men of valour put to flight, and the grinding maids are idle, because reduced to a few, then shall the maids who look out at the windows be darkened,
[Ecclesiastes] [12]:[4] and the doors of the market place will be shut at the feebleness o the voice of the grinding maid, which will be raised to the plaintive tone of the young ostrich; and all the daughters of the song will be brought low;
[Ecclesiastes] [12]:[5] and they will look up to the height and there are terrors in the way. Though the almond tree shall have bloomed and the locust hath been fattened and the caper bush is shed, because the man is gone to his long home, therefore the mourners have made a circuit in the market place.
[Ecclesiastes] [12]:[6] Until the silver cord can be wound up no more, and the golden bowl be broken, and the bucket broken at the well, and the wheel on the well shall run down with it,
[Ecclesiastes] [12]:[7] and dust return to dust as it was, and the spirit return to God who gave it;
[Ecclesiastes] [12]:[8] vanity of vanities, said the preacher; all things are vanity.
[Ecclesiastes] [12]:[9] And moreover, because the preacher was wise, because he taught mankind wisdom; that the ear might find what is comely from parables,
[Ecclesiastes] [12]:[10] the preacher made diligent search to find pleasing words and a writing of rectitude—words of truth.
[Ecclesiastes] [12]:[11] The words of the wise are like goads and nails ready made, which have been laid up in repositories from one shepherd to another.
[Ecclesiastes] [12]:[12] What remaineth of them, my son, keep thou. There is no end of making many books. And much study is a weariness to the flesh.
[Ecclesiastes] [12]:[13] As a conclusion of the discourse, hear thou the whole sum and substance.— Fear God and keep his commandments: for this conclusion every man should draw,
[Ecclesiastes] [12]:[14] That God will bring the whole work into judgment in every case not taken notice of, whether it be good or whether it be evil.
[Song of Solomon] [1]:[1] A Song of Songs which is Solomons.
[Song of Solomon] [1]:[2] Let him kiss me with the kisses of his mouth. Because thy breasts are better than wine;
[Song of Solomon] [1]:[3] and the fragrance of thine odours is above all perfumes—Thy name out-poured oil, therefore the virgins have loved thee.
[Song of Solomon] [1]:[4] Thou art drawn: after thee we will run to the fragrance of thy perfumes. The king brought me into his inner apartment. Let us rejoice and be gladdened by thee. We will love thy breasts better than wine: rectitude hath loved thee.
[Song of Solomon] [1]:[5] I am brown but comely, O daughters of Jerusalem; like the tents of Kedar—like the curtains of Solomon.
[Song of Solomon] [1]:[6] Look not on me, because I am of a dark complexion—Because the sun hath looked upon me. My mother's children quarrelled with me—They made me a keeper in the vineyards. Mine own vineyard I did not watch.
[Song of Solomon] [1]:[7] Tell me thou whom my soul loveth, where thou tendest thy flock—Where thou causest it to rest at noon, perhaps I may be like a wanderer among the tents of thy companions.
[Song of Solomon] [1]:[8] If thou wouldst not make thyself known, O fairest of women; go out by thyself in the track of the flock; and feed thy kids by the shepherds tents.
[Song of Solomon] [1]:[9] To that steed of mine in Pharao's chariot I have compared thee, my love!
[Song of Solomon] [1]:[10] What! thy cheeks are beautiful as a turtle dove's: and thy neck as strings of pearls.
[Song of Solomon] [1]:[11] We will make for thee suitable figures of gold with spangles of silver.
[Song of Solomon] [1]:[12] While the king was at his banquet, my spikenard sent forth its odour.
[Song of Solomon] [1]:[13] My dear brother is to me a nosegay of stacte it shall be lodged in my bosom.
[Song of Solomon] [1]:[14] My dear brother is to me a cluster of the cypress, in the vineyards of Engaddi.
[Song of Solomon] [1]:[15] Behold thou art fair, my love; behold thou art fair. Thine eyes are doves.
[Song of Solomon] [1]:[16] Behold thou art comely, my dear brother—beautiful indeed! Near our bed thou wast shaded.
[Song of Solomon] [1]:[17] The beams of our houses are cedar. Cypress our vaulted cielings.
[Song of Solomon] [2]:[1] I am a flower of the field, and a lilly of the vallies.
[Song of Solomon] [2]:[2] As a lilly among thorns, so is my consort among the daughters.
[Song of Solomon] [2]:[3] As the citron tree among the trees of the forests; so is my dear brother among the youths. In its shade I took great delight and sat, and its fruit was sweet to my taste.
[Song of Solomon] [2]:[4] O conduct me to the house of wine, and order for me what I love.
[Song of Solomon] [2]:[5] Strengthen with perfumes—strew around me citrons, for I am wounded with love.
[Song of Solomon] [2]:[6] Let his left hand be under my head, and his right embrace me!
[Song of Solomon] [2]:[7] I have adjured you, O daughters of Jerusalem, by the powers and virtues of the field, that you stir not up nor awake my love till she please.
[Song of Solomon] [2]:[8] It is the voice of my dear brother! lo he cometh, bounding over the mountains—skipping over the hills. My dear brother is like a roe; or a young hart on the mountains of Baithor.
[Song of Solomon] [2]:[9] Lo! he is behind our wall, looking in at the windows, taking a near view through the lattice.
[Song of Solomon] [2]:[10] My dear brother addressing me saith, "Arise my love, my fair one, my dove! For lo! the winter is past—the rain is over: is gone.
[Song of Solomon] [2]:[11] The flowers appear on the earth—the pruning season is come.
[Song of Solomon] [2]:[12] The voice of the turtle is heard in our land.
[Song of Solomon] [2]:[13] The fig tree hath put forth its green figs: the vines are in bloom—they have shed a fragrance.
[Song of Solomon] [2]:[14] Arise, come my beloved, my fair one, my dove. Yes, come thou dove of mine: in the shelter of the rock near the wall. Shew me thy face and let me hear thy voice: for thy voice is sweet and thy countenance comely."
[Song of Solomon] [2]:[15] Catch for us the little foxes, that spoil vines: for our vines are in blossom.
[Song of Solomon] [2]:[16] My dear brother is mine and I am his: he is feeding among the lillies.
[Song of Solomon] [2]:[17] Till the day blow fresh and the shades be removed. Return—Be, O my dear brother, like a roe, or a young hart on the caverned mountains.
[Song of Solomon] [3]:[1] By night on my bed I sought him whom my soul loved. I sought him but did not find him. I called him but he made me no answer.
[Song of Solomon] [3]:[2] I will rise now and take a circuit in the city in the places of publick resort and in the streets; and seek him whom my soul loveth. I sought him but did not find him.
[Song of Solomon] [3]:[3] The watchmen who take their rounds in the city, met me. Saw ye him whom my soul loveth?
[Song of Solomon] [3]:[4] It was but a little while after I parted from them, till I found him whom my soul loveth. I took hold of him and did not let him go till I brought him to my mother's house and into the chambers of her who conceived me.
[Song of Solomon] [3]:[5] I charged you, O daughters of Jerusalem by the powers and virtues of the field, not to stir up nor awake my love till he please.
[Song of Solomon] [3]:[6] What is this coming up from the wilderness; like a column of smoke, fuming with myrrh and incense made of all the perfumer's powders?
[Song of Solomon] [3]:[7] It is the palanquin of Solomon, three score chiefs of the chiefs of Israel are around it.
[Song of Solomon] [3]:[8] All swordsmen expert in war. Every man with his sword on his thigh, for fear of danger by night.
[Song of Solomon] [3]:[9] King Solomon had made himself a palanquin of the wood of Libanus.
[Song of Solomon] [3]:[10] Its columns he made of silver, and his sofa was of gold. Its canopy was purple, and its inside carpeted with love, for the daughters of Jerusalem.
[Song of Solomon] [3]:[11] O daughter of Sion go forth and view king Solomon; in the crown with which his mother crowned him; on the day of his espousals—Even on the day of the gladness of his heart.
[Song of Solomon] [4]:[1] Behold, thou art fair, my love! Behold thou art fair! Thine eyes are doves, now thy veil is removed! thy hair is like the flocks of goats which are seen from mount Gralaad:
[Song of Solomon] [4]:[2] thy teeth like the flocks of shorn sheep; which have just come up from the washing pool: they are all twins and there is not one defective among them.
[Song of Solomon] [4]:[3] Thy lips are like a braid of scarlet and thy speech is graceful: as the skin of a pomegranate, so are thy cheeks, now thy veil is removed.
[Song of Solomon] [4]:[4] Thy neck is like the tower of David, which was built for an armoury: on it are hung a thousand shields; all the javelins of the worthies.
[Song of Solomon] [4]:[5] Thy two breasts are like two twin fawns of a roe; which are browzing among lillies.
[Song of Solomon] [4]:[6] Till the day breeze spring up and the shades are removed, I will go by myself to the mountain of myrrh, and to the hill of frankincense.
[Song of Solomon] [4]:[7] Thou art altogether fair, my love and there is no blemish in thee.
[Song of Solomon] [4]:[8] Come from Libanus, my spouse; come from Libanus. Thou canst come, yes come safely from the top of Pistis—from the summit of Sanir and Herinon—from lions dens, from the leopards mountains.
[Song of Solomon] [4]:[9] Thou hast ravished my heart, my sister spouse: thou hast ravished my heart with a glance of thine eyes—with an endearing turn of thy neck.
[Song of Solomon] [4]:[10] How beautiful are thy breasts, my sister spouse! how much more pleasing than wine are thy breasts! and the smell of thy garments, than all spices!
[Song of Solomon] [4]:[11] thy lips, my spouse, drop as a honey comb: honey and milk flow from thy tongue: and the fragrance of thy garments is like the smell of Lebanon.
[Song of Solomon] [4]:[12] Thou art an enclosed garden, my sister spouse: a close shut garden; a sealed fountain.
[Song of Solomon] [4]:[13] Thy productions are a paradise of pomegranates with delicious fruits; aromatic plants with the varieties of balsams—spikenard and saffron, sweet cane and cinnamon;
[Song of Solomon] [4]:[14] with all kinds of trees of incense—myrrh, aloth with all the principal spices.
[Song of Solomon] [4]:[15] A garden fountain and a well of living water, flowing with gentle murmurs from Libanus.
[Song of Solomon] [4]:[16] Awake O north wind, come also, O south wind and blow gently through my garden, and let my aromatic spices flow. Let my dear brother come down to his garden; and taste the delicious fruits thereof.
[Song of Solomon] [5]:[1] I have come to my garden, my sister spouse: I have gathered my myrrh with my spices: I have eaten my bread with my honey; I have drunk my wine with my milk. Eat, O friends; drink—yes drink plentifully O brethren.
[Song of Solomon] [5]:[2] I sleep; but my heart is awake. The voice of my dear brother! he knocketh at the door! "Open to me, my sister, my dove, my consecrated one! for my head is filled with dew, and my locks with the drops of the night."
[Song of Solomon] [5]:[3] I have put off my garment; how can I put it on? I have washed my feet; how can I defile them?
[Song of Solomon] [5]:[4] My dear brother withdrew his hand from the hole in the door. Whereupon my bowels yearned for him.
[Song of Solomon] [5]:[5] I rose to open for my dear brother; my hands dropped myrrh; and my fingers myrrh plenteously on the handles of the bar.
[Song of Solomon] [5]:[6] I opened for my dear brother. He was gone! my soul melted for the speech of him. I sought him but did not find him: I called him but he made me no answer.
[Song of Solomon] [5]:[7] They who go the rounds in the city met me. They smote me—they wounded me. The keepers of the walls took my veil from me.
[Song of Solomon] [5]:[8] I adjured you, O daughters of Jerusalem by the powers and virtues of the field. If you found my dear brother—What should you tell him? Tell him that I am sick of love.
[Song of Solomon] [5]:[9] "What is thy dear brother more than another brother, O fairest of women? What is thy dear brother more than another brother, that thou hast thus adjured us?"
[Song of Solomon] [5]:[10] My dear brother is white and ruddy, he is the chiefest among tens of thousands.
[Song of Solomon] [5]:[11] His head is gold of Kephas; his flowing locks black as a raven.
[Song of Solomon] [5]:[12] His eyes are like doves by streams of water—milk white doves sitting by streams.
[Song of Solomon] [5]:[13] His cheeks are like beds of spices emitting perfumes; his lips are lillies distilling myrrh plenteously.
[Song of Solomon] [5]:[14] His hands are of gold, finely turned, beset with chrysolites: his body, an ivory cabinet on a sapphire stone:
[Song of Solomon] [5]:[15] his legs, pillars of marble fixed on pedestals of gold: his countenance like Libanus, majestic as the cedars his mouth is sweetness itself.
[Song of Solomon] [5]:[16] He is altogether lovely. Such is my dear brother, and such my love, O daughters of Jerusalem.
[Song of Solomon] [6]:[1] "Where did thy dear brother go, O fairest of women? Whither did thy dear brother bend his course; that we may seek him with thee?"
[Song of Solomon] [6]:[2] My dear brother went down to his garden to the beds of spices; to feed in the garden and to gather lillies.
[Song of Solomon] [6]:[3] I am my dear brother's and he is mine; he is feeding among the lillies!
[Song of Solomon] [6]:[4] Thou my consort, art beautiful as Terzah; comely as Jerusalem; dazzling as embattled hosts!
[Song of Solomon] [6]:[5] Turn thine eyes aside from me for they have transported me. Thy hair is like a flock of goats which are seen on mount Galaad:
[Song of Solomon] [6]:[6] thy teeth like flocks of shorn sheep, just come up from washing: all twins and there is not one defective among them: thy lips are like a braid of scarlet, and thy speech is graceful.
[Song of Solomon] [6]:[7] Thy cheek is like the downy skin of a pomegranate now thy veil is removed.
[Song of Solomon] [6]:[8] There are sixty queens and eighty concubines, and virgins without number: one is my dove, my consecrated one.
[Song of Solomon] [6]:[9] One is her mother's only child—the darling of her who bore her. Daughters viewed her—nay queens and even concubines will hail her happy and thus extoll her.
[Song of Solomon] [6]:[10] "Who is she that is looking out like the morning; fair as the moon, distinguishable as the sun, and dazzling as embattled hosts."
[Song of Solomon] [6]:[11] I went down to the garden of nuts to look at the productions of the valley: to seek whether the vine had budded or the pomegranate blossomed:
[Song of Solomon] [6]:[12] I am there to grant thee my breasts. My soul did not recollect this. It made me like the chariots of Aminadab.
[Song of Solomon] [6]:[13] Return, return, O Sunamite! return, return that we may see thee! What would you see in the Sunamite, who is coming like the moving bands of an army?
[Song of Solomon] [7]:[1] How beautiful were thy steps in thy sandals, daughter of Nadab? The infoldings of thy loins are like jewellery, the work of an artist:
[Song of Solomon] [7]:[2] thy clasp is a well turned goblet, not destitute of mingled wine: thy body is like a heap of wheat encompassed about with lillies:
[Song of Solomon] [7]:[3] thy two breasts are like two twin fawns of a roe; thy neck is like a tower of ivory.
[Song of Solomon] [7]:[4] Thine eyes like the pools at Esebon by the gates of the daughter of many: thy nose is like the tower of Lebanon which looketh towards Damascus:
[Song of Solomon] [7]:[5] thy head is like Carmel and the tresses of thy head like Purpura. The king is detained in the antichamber
[Song of Solomon] [7]:[6] How beautiful thou art! and how sweet! O my love! how delightful!
[Song of Solomon] [7]:[7] In respect to thy stateliness, thou hast been compared to the palm tree, and thy breasts to its clusters.
[Song of Solomon] [7]:[8] I said, I will climb the palm tree—I will clasp its topmost boughs; and thy breasts shall be now like the clusters of the vine; and the smell of thy nose like citrons; and the roof of thy mouth like choice wine—
[Song of Solomon] [7]:[9] Which is poured out rightly for my dear brother properly adapted with my lips and my teeth.
[Song of Solomon] [7]:[10] I am my dear brother's and towards me is his desire.
[Song of Solomon] [7]:[11] Come, my dear brother, let us go out to the fields.
[Song of Solomon] [7]:[12] Let us lodge in the villages: let us get up early to the vineyards: let us see whether the vine is budded: whether the young grape has made its appearance: whether the pomegranates are in blossom. There I will grant thee my breasts.
[Song of Solomon] [7]:[13] The mandrakes have shed a fragrance: and in our hoards are all manner of delicious fruits, newly gathered as well as old. For thee, my dear brother I have kept them.
[Song of Solomon] [8]:[1] O that thou, my dear brother, hadst sucked the breasts of my mother; meeting thee abroad I could kiss thee and not be despised;
[Song of Solomon] [8]:[2] I could take thee and conduct thee to my mother's house: and to the inmost apartment of her who conceived me, I could cause thee to drink spiced wine of the juice of my pomegranates;
[Song of Solomon] [8]:[3] his left hand might be under my head and his right embrace me.
[Song of Solomon] [8]:[4] I have charged you, O daughters of Jerusalem, by the powers and virtues of the field; that you stir not up nor awake my love till she please.
[Song of Solomon] [8]:[5] Who is this coming up cloathed in white leaning on her beloved? Under a citron tree I awaked thee. It was there thy mother bare thee. There she who brought thee forth suffered the pangs of childbirth.
[Song of Solomon] [8]:[6] O set me as a seal on thy heart—as a seal on thine arm: for love is strong as death; jealousy inflexible as Hades: its wings are wings of fire its flames—
[Song of Solomon] [8]:[7] Much water cannot quench love; nor can floods drown it. Though a man give all his substance for love, he may be thoroughly despised.
[Song of Solomon] [8]:[8] Our sister is little and hath no breasts. What can we do for our sister on the day when she shall be demanded in marriage.
[Song of Solomon] [8]:[9] If she be a wall we will build on her silver turrets, and if she be a door we will line her with boards of cedar.
[Song of Solomon] [8]:[10] I am a wall and my breasts are turrets. I was in their view as one who had found favour.
[Song of Solomon] [8]:[11] Solomon had a vineyard at Beelamon, he let this vineyard of his to tenants, a man is to give him a thousand pieces of silver for its fruit.
[Song of Solomon] [8]:[12] My vineyard which is before me shall continue to be mine. Solomon shall have the thousand pieces of silver, and they who take care of its fruits, two hundred.
[Song of Solomon] [8]:[13] Thou who art sitting in the gardens, companions are waiting thy voice. Let me hear it—
[Song of Solomon] [8]:[14] Away, my dear brother, and be like a roe; or a young hart on the mountains of spices.
[Lamentations] [1]:[1] How solitary sitteth the city which was full of people! she who was abundant among nations is become like a widow! the empress of provinces is become tributary!
[Lamentations] [1]:[2] She wept sore by night and her tears are on her cheeks and of all her lovers there is none to comfort her. All her friends dealt treacherously with her. They became her enemies.
[Lamentations] [1]:[3] Judea is forced to emigrate. On account of her humiliation and because of her great servitude, she sat down among the nations; she found no rest. All her persecutors found her among them who were afflicting her.
[Lamentations] [1]:[4] The highways of Sion mourn; because none attend her festivals. All her gates are desolate. Her priests groan bitterly. Her virgins are carried away; and she is by herself in bitterness.
[Lamentations] [1]:[5] They who afflict her are become her head; and her enemies prosper. Because the Lord hath humbled her for the multitude of her transgressions; her children are gone into captivity before the oppressor.
[Lamentations] [1]:[6] From the daughter of Sion all her comeliness is taken away. Her chiefs were like rams which could find no pasture; and they came without strength in view of the pursuer.
[Lamentations] [1]:[7] In the days of her humiliation and rejection, Jerusalem remembered all the good things which she had in days of old. When her people fell into the hands of the oppressor, and there was none to help her; her enemies seeing this, laughed at her festivals held in commemoration of her settlement.
[Lamentations] [1]:[8] Jerusalem sinned grievously, therefore she became unstable as a wave. By all that honoured her she hath been humbled, for they saw her nakedness, while she sighing turned away her face.
[Lamentations] [1]:[9] Her uncleanness was before her feet. She was insensible to her extreme distress. He hath indeed lowered her haughtiness; there is none who comforteth her. Lord, behold my affliction because the enemy is magnified.
[Lamentations] [1]:[10] On all the objects of her desire the oppressor hath laid his hand; for into her sanctuary she saw nations entering, concerning whom thou hast commanded that they should not come into thy congregation.
[Lamentations] [1]:[11] All her people were sighing deeply, seeking bread. They gave up the objects of her desire, for meat to preserve life. Look down, O Lord, and see how she is dishonoured.
[Lamentations] [1]:[12] Ho! all ye who travel the way turn and see! is there any sorrow like this of mine, which he hath denounced against me. The Lord hath humbled me in the day of his fierce indignation.
[Lamentations] [1]:[13] From his loftiness he hath sent a fire; he brought it in among my bones. He hath spread a net for my feet and turned me backwards. He hath made me desolate and full of sorrow, all the live long day.
[Lamentations] [1]:[14] He hath kept a watch on my transgressions. They are wreathed round my hands. They have reached up to my neck. My strength is enfeebled. For into my hands the Lord hath sent pains. I cannot stand.
[Lamentations] [1]:[15] From amidst me the Lord hath moved all my mighty men. He hath brought upon me the time for crushing my chosen bands. The Lord trod out a vat for the virgin daughter of Juda.
[Lamentations] [1]:[16] For these things I mourn. Mine eye hath flowed with water, because my comforter, he who is the supporter of my life, is far removed from me. My children are vanished because the enemy hath prevailed.
[Lamentations] [1]:[17] Sion stretched forth her hand; there is none to comfort her. The Lord gave a command respecting Jacob; around him are they who afflict him. Jerusalem became among them, like one set apart as unclean.
[Lamentations] [1]:[18] The Lord is righteous; for against his express commandment I rebelled. Hear I beseech you all ye peoples and behold my sorrow! my virgins and my young men are gone into captivity.
[Lamentations] [1]:[19] I called on my lovers; but they with false excuses deceived me. My priests and my elders expired in the city, because they sought food to support their lives, but found none.
[Lamentations] [1]:[20] Look, Lord! because of my distress my bowels are troubled; and my heart within me is distracted, because I have grievously rebelled. Abroad the sword, and at home death have bereaved me of children.
[Lamentations] [1]:[21] Hear, I beseech thee! because I groan; there is none to comfort me. All mine enemies heard of my calamities, and rejoiced at what thou hast done. Thou hast brought on a day: thou hast proclaimed a time.
[Lamentations] [1]:[22] They were like me, let all their wickedness come before thee. Make search for them as they have made search for all my transgressions. For my groans are many, and my heart is afflicted with grief.
[Lamentations] [2]:[1] Oh! how the Lord hath in his wrath covered with darkness the daughter of Sion! he hath cast down, from heaven, upon the earth, the glory of Israel; and hath not remembered his own footstool.
[Lamentations] [2]:[2] In the day of his indignation the Lord overwhelmed and did not spare; in his wrath he destroyed all the comeliness of Jacob: he levelled to the ground the strong holds of the daughter of Juda. He brought contempt on her king, and her chiefs.
[Lamentations] [2]:[3] In his fierce anger he broke the whole horn of Israel: he turned back his right hand from before his enemy: he kindled in Jacob a flaming fire, which devoured all around.
[Lamentations] [2]:[4] He bent his bow like an opposing enemy—like an adversary he strengthened his right hand and slew all the desires of mine eyes. In the tent of the daughter of Sion he poured out his wrath like fire.
[Lamentations] [2]:[5] The Lord became like an enemy. He overwhelmed Israel. He sunk her palaces. He destroyed his own munitions, and multiplied on the daughter of Juda humiliation on humiliation.
[Lamentations] [2]:[6] He laid open like a vineyard his own dwelling; he marred his own festivals. The Lord forgot the festivals and sabbaths which he had established in Sion, and by the rebuke of his anger vexed king and priest and chief.
[Lamentations] [2]:[7] The Lord hath cast off his altar; he hath shaken off his sanctuary. With the hand of an enemy he hath demolished the wall of her palaces. They uttered a shout in the house of the Lord as on the day of a festival.
[Lamentations] [2]:[8] He turned back to destroy the wall of the daughter of Sion; he stretched out a measuring line: he withdrew not his hand from destruction; so the outer wall mourned and the inner wall languished with it.
[Lamentations] [2]:[9] Her gates were dashed to the ground: he destroyed and broke to pieces her bars, her king and her chiefs among the nations. Law is no more; and her prophets saw not a vision from the Lord.
[Lamentations] [2]:[10] The elders of the daughter of Sion sat on the ground. They were silent. They heaped dust on their heads; they girded themselves with sackcloth. He humbled to the ground the virgin princesses in Jerusalem.
[Lamentations] [2]:[11] Mine eyes failed with tears; my heart was astounded. My glory was poured on the ground for the distress of the daughter of my people when the infant and suckling expired in the streets of the city.
